Oh, well... I wouldn't say it ruins my life, Doctor. I really wouldn't. I mean, I still keep up with all my responsibilities! I go to Mass every Sunday, I still go to my choir practices, and my house is always neat and tidy, I promise you that. I don't let it stop me from doing what I need to do.

I might not answer the phone if a friend calls, just because... well, I feel so silly and embarrassed about what I've done. I just want to be alone until the feeling passes. But that's not too awful, is it? I mean, everyone needs a quiet evening to themselves sometimes, don't they? I hope you don't think it means I'm falling apart, Doctor. Oh, my... well, I've always prided myself on having good manners, Doctor. I really have. All my life I've been such a slow eater, taking my time like a proper lady should. 

But... oh dear, I suppose I need to be completely honest with you. Lately, when... when this happens, I do find myself rushing. I don't even sit down at the table. I'll just be standing right there by the kitchen counter, and... well, I eat so fast. I hardly even taste the food before the next bite is already in my mouth. 

It sounds just terrible, doesn't it? I feel so unladylike and embarrassed even admitting it out loud to you. But I promise you, it's only during those little spells. Any other time, I sit down with a proper plate and napkin. I do hope you don't think I've lost all my dignity... I really am a very proper person normally, Doctor.

Oh, no, no... I wouldn't say I hide away to do it, Doctor. I really wouldn't want you to think I'm being sneaky or... or deceitful in any way. I've always tried to be a very open and honest person.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, dear... well, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or anything quite so dramatic as depressed, Doctor. I really try to keep a positive outlook on life! I count my blessings every day, I truly do. 

But... well, if I'm being perfectly honest, which I always try to be with you... I do feel a bit sad afterward. And, well, a little ashamed. You see, cooking and eating... it always used to be about family for me. It was about caring for my late husband and the children, putting a nice meal on the table. And now... well, it feels like I've turned something that was so lovely and full of love into... into this thing I do alone. 

It makes me feel like I have a secret I need to keep from the ladies at church, and I just hate feeling that way. I've always been such an open book. It's just a silly bit of sadness, though. I usually brush myself off and get right back to my day. That's the right way to handle it, isn't it, Doctor? I don't want you to think I'm sitting around feeling sorry for myself all day.

Oh, goodness, no... I wouldn't say that at all, Doctor. I really try my best not to let my moods get the better of me. I've always believed that you just have to put on a brave face and keep busy, you know?

I feel a bit foolish admitting it, I really do. I don't want you to think badly of me, but... yes. \n\nBy the time I finally manage to stop, my stomach does feel quite tight and heavy. I usually have to go sit back in my recliner and... well, loosen my waistband just a little bit, because I went past what felt comfortable. I know it sounds terrible for a woman my age to let herself get to that point, and I really am ashamed of it. I'm sorry, is that the kind of detail you need, Doctor? I just want to make sure I'm being helpful.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, after these episodes of eating more than you intended, do you ever try to make up for it by doing something like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, Doctor, I would never do anything like that. I mean... making myself sick or taking pills like that, oh dear, no. That sounds so dreadful. \n\nI just... well, I just sit in my chair until the feeling passes. I might skip my next meal or just have a little cup of tea later on, but that's all. I don't do any of those extreme things, I promise. I know I eat too much sometimes, but I wouldn't do anything unnatural to my body afterward. I hope that's a good thing? I mean, I hope that's the right answer... I'm trying to be very honest with you.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, how often do these episodes of eating more than you intend happen? Would you say they occur several times a week, once a week, or less often?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, I certainly don't want you to think I'm just sitting around the house eating all day long. It's not like that, I promise. But... well, since my dear friend passed away, I suppose it has been happening most weeks. \n\nJust... maybe once or twice a week, really. It always seems to be on those quiet afternoons, you know? Like after I come home from choir, or when I finish up a bit of gardening, and the house is just so still... I don't mean to do it, and it really is just once or twice. Is... is that a very terrible amount, Doctor? I do hope I'm not doing too badly. I really am trying my best to be good about it.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, do these episodes of eating more than you intend cause you a lot of distress, or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I wouldn't say it ruins my life, Doctor. I really wouldn't. I mean, I still keep up with all my responsibilities! I go to Mass every Sunday, I still go to my choir practices, and my house is always neat and tidy, I promise you that. I don't let it stop me from doing what I need to do.\n\nBut... well, I suppose it does bother me a little bit. Sometimes, if I've had one of those... those afternoons where I eat too much, I might just stay inside for the rest of the day. I might not answer the phone if a friend calls, just because... well, I feel so silly and embarrassed about what I've done. I just want to be alone until the feeling passes. But that's not too awful, is it? I mean, everyone needs a quiet evening to themselves sometimes, don't they? I hope you don't think it means I'm falling apart, Doctor. I'm really doing quite well otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes when you eat more than you intend, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... well, I've always prided myself on having good manners, Doctor. I really have. All my life I've been such a slow eater, taking my time like a proper lady should. \n\nBut... oh dear, I suppose I need to be completely honest with you. Lately, when... when this happens, I do find myself rushing. I don't even sit down at the table. I'll just be standing right there by the kitchen counter, and... well, I eat so fast. I hardly even taste the food before the next bite is already in my mouth. \n\nIt sounds just terrible, doesn't it? I feel so unladylike and embarrassed even admitting it out loud to you. But I promise you, it's only during those little spells. Any other time, I sit down with a proper plate and napkin. I do hope you don't think I've lost all my dignity... I really am a very proper person normally, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, when these episodes happen, do you tend to eat alone because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no... I wouldn't say I hide away to do it, Doctor. I really wouldn't want you to think I'm being sneaky or... or deceitful in any way. I've always tried to be a very open and honest person. \n\nIt's just that... well, I live by myself now, you see. So I'm almost always alone in the house anyway when it happens. It's not that I'm waiting for people to leave, or locking the doors because I'm ashamed of the amount... it just sort of happens when it's just me and the quiet. If a neighbor were to pop by right then, I would certainly stop and put the kettle on for tea, like a proper hostess. I don't shut the blinds or do anything dreadful like that. \n\nThat makes sense, doesn't it? I do hope you believe me that I'm not trying to be secretive. I'd hate for you to think badly of me, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, aside from feeling embarrassed afterward, do these episodes ever make you feel anxious, guilty, or depressed while they’re happening or afterward?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or anything quite so dramatic as depressed, Doctor. I really try to keep a positive outlook on life! I count my blessings every day, I truly do. \n\nBut... well, if I'm being perfectly honest, which I always try to be with you... I do feel a bit sad afterward. And, well, a little ashamed. You see, cooking and eating... it always used to be about family for me. It was about caring for my late husband and the children, putting a nice meal on the table. And now... well, it feels like I've turned something that was so lovely and full of love into... into this thing I do alone. \n\nIt makes me feel like I have a secret I need to keep from the ladies at church, and I just hate feeling that way. I've always been such an open book. It's just a silly bit of sadness, though. I usually brush myself off and get right back to my day. That's the right way to handle it, isn't it, Doctor? I don't want you to think I'm sitting around feeling sorry for myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, do these episodes of eating more than you intend tend to happen when you're feeling especially sad, lonely, or upset?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... I wouldn't say that at all, Doctor. I really try my best not to let my moods get the better of me. I've always believed that you just have to put on a brave face and keep busy, you know? \n\nI don't... I don't sit down and eat just because I'm feeling a bit lonely or down. That sounds so... well, so weak, doesn't it? And I really do pride myself on being strong. I keep up with my knitting, I watch my programs... I don't let myself wallow. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say there's any sort of connection like that. It just... happens sometimes, out of the blue. It's not because I'm upset. I do hope you believe me on that, Doctor. I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who can't handle her own feelings... because I handle them quite well, don't I?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh... well, I suppose... I mean, I wouldn't call it a "very large amount," Doctor. That makes it sound so... gluttonous, doesn't it? I really am quite sensible most of the time. I wouldn't want you to picture me just endlessly stuffing myself. 

But... well, sometimes, say, on a Tuesday afternoon after choir practice, or if I've been out working in the garden and I've worked up a bit of an appetite... I might fix myself a nice plate of rice and beans, or maybe some of my chicken stew. And then... well, I might just go back for a little more. And perhaps a bit more after that. 

Before I realize it, I suppose I might have eaten what you could call... well, maybe enough for two or three meals, just in that afternoon. But it's only because the food is there and it tastes so good, you know? It's not like I'm trying to be greedy. That's just a normal thing to do when you've had a busy day, isn't it, Doctor? I'm sure plenty of people have a hearty appetite after gardening. You don't think too poorly of me for that, do you?

I've always tried to be a very open and honest person. \n\nIt's just that... well, I live by myself now, you see. So I'm almost always alone in the house anyway when it happens. It's not that I'm waiting for people to leave, or locking the doors because I'm ashamed of the amount... it just sort of happens when it's just me and the quiet. If a neighbor were to pop by right then, I would certainly stop and put the kettle on for tea, like a proper hostess. I don't shut the blinds or do anything dreadful like that. \n\nThat makes sense, doesn't it? I do hope you believe me that I'm not trying to be secretive. I'd hate for you to think badly of me, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, aside from feeling embarrassed afterward, do these episodes ever make you feel anxious, guilty, or depressed while they’re happening or afterward?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or anything quite so dramatic as depressed, Doctor. I really try to keep a positive outlook on life! I count my blessings every day, I truly do. \n\nBut... well, if I'm being perfectly honest, which I always try to be with you... I do feel a bit sad afterward. And, well, a little ashamed. You see, cooking and eating... it always used to be about family for me. It was about caring for my late husband and the children, putting a nice meal on the table. And now... well, it feels like I've turned something that was so lovely and full of love into... into this thing I do alone. \n\nIt makes me feel like I have a secret I need to keep from the ladies at church, and I just hate feeling that way. I've always been such an open book. It's just a silly bit of sadness, though. I usually brush myself off and get right back to my day. That's the right way to handle it, isn't it, Doctor? I don't want you to think I'm sitting around feeling sorry for myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, do these episodes of eating more than you intend tend to happen when you're feeling especially sad, lonely, or upset?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... I wouldn't say that at all, Doctor. I really try my best not to let my moods get the better of me. I've always believed that you just have to put on a brave face and keep busy, you know? \n\nI don't... I don't sit down and eat just because I'm feeling a bit lonely or down. That sounds so... well, so weak, doesn't it? And I really do pride myself on being strong. I keep up with my knitting, I watch my programs... I don't let myself wallow. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say there's any sort of connection like that. It just... happens sometimes, out of the blue. It's not because I'm upset. I do hope you believe me on that, Doctor. I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who can't handle her own feelings... because I handle them quite well, don't I?\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes when you eat more than you intend, do you ever notice that you're eating a very large amount of food in a short time, like within just a couple of hours?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, I suppose... I mean, I wouldn't call it a \"very large amount,\" Doctor. That makes it sound so... gluttonous, doesn't it? I really am quite sensible most of the time. I wouldn't want you to picture me just endlessly stuffing myself. \n\nBut... well, sometimes, say, on a Tuesday afternoon after choir practice, or if I've been out working in the garden and I've worked up a bit of an appetite... I might fix myself a nice plate of rice and beans, or maybe some of my chicken stew. And then... well, I might just go back for a little more. And perhaps a bit more after that. \n\nBefore I realize it, I suppose I might have eaten what you could call... well, maybe enough for two or three meals, just in that afternoon. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, distress? Goodness, no. I wouldn't use a word like that, Doctor. That sounds so... well, so heavy and serious. 

Like I mentioned before, I really just try to brush myself off. I don't let things linger or ruin my day. Once the kitchen is cleaned up and the dishes are put away, I just... I put it out of my mind. I might say a little prayer, and then I get right back to my knitting or my chores. It really doesn't stay with me at all. 

I've always believed it's best not to dwell on things, you know? If you sit around worrying, you just make yourself miserable, and I've always tried to be a cheerful person for the people around me. It's much better to just keep moving forward with a smile and focus on the good things. That's the healthiest way to be, don't you think? I wouldn't want you to worry that I'm at home agonizing over things.

It's probably just a silly habit, but... is that the sort of thing I should be telling you? I just want to make sure I'm answering your question right.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, when you find yourself continuing to eat even though you’ve told yourself to stop, does it feel like you’ve lost control over your eating during those moments?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, Doctor... I suppose that's exactly it. It's just... oh, I hate to make a fuss about it, but yes. I lose control. It's not that I'm trying to be gluttonous, I promise... it's just like my brain and my hands aren't talking to each other for a little while. I hope that makes sense? I'm trying my best to explain it properly for you.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, when these episodes happen, do you find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full, or eating a lot even when you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, I suppose I do. I feel a bit foolish admitting it, I really do. I don't want you to think badly of me, but... yes. \n\nBy the time I finally manage to stop, my stomach does feel quite tight and heavy. I usually have to go sit back in my recliner and... well, loosen my waistband just a little bit, because I went past what felt comfortable. I just want to make sure I'm being helpful.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, after these episodes of eating more than you intended, do you ever try to make up for it by doing something like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, Doctor, I would never do anything like that. I mean... making myself sick or taking pills like that, oh dear, no. That sounds so dreadful. \n\nI just... well, I just sit in my chair until the feeling passes. I might skip my next meal or just have a little cup of tea later on, but that's all. I don't do any of those extreme things, I promise. I know I eat too much sometimes, but I wouldn't do anything unnatural to my body afterward. I hope that's a good thing? I mean, I hope that's the right answer... I'm trying to be very honest with you.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, how often do these episodes of eating more than you intend happen? Would you say they occur several times a week, once a week, or less often?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, I certainly don't want you to think I'm just sitting around the house eating all day long. It's not like that, I promise. But... well, since my dear friend passed away, I suppose it has been happening most weeks. \n\nJust... maybe once or twice a week, really. It always seems to be on those quiet afternoons, you know? Like after I come home from choir, or when I finish up a bit of gardening, and the house is just so still... I don't mean to do it, and it really is just once or twice. Is... is that a very terrible amount, Doctor? I do hope I'm not doing too badly. I really am trying my best to be good about it.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, do these episodes of eating more than you intend cause you a lot of distress, or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I wouldn't say it ruins my life, Doctor. I really wouldn't. I mean, I still keep up with all my responsibilities! I go to Mass every Sunday, I still go to my choir practices, and my house is always neat and tidy, I promise you that. I don't let it stop me from doing what I need to do.\n\nBut... well, I suppose it does bother me a little bit. Sometimes, if I've had one of those... those afternoons where I eat too much, I might just stay inside for the rest of the day. I might not answer the phone if a friend calls, just because... well, I feel so silly and embarrassed about what I've done. I just want to be alone until the feeling passes. But that's not too awful, is it? I mean, everyone needs a quiet evening to themselves sometimes, don't they? I hope you don't think it means I'm falling apart, Doctor. I'm really doing quite well otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes when you eat more than you intend, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... well, I've always prided myself on having good manners, Doctor. I really have. All my life I've been such a slow eater, taking my time like a proper lady should. \n\nBut... oh dear, I suppose I need to be completely honest with you. Lately, when... when this happens, I do find myself rushing. I don't even sit down at the table. I'll just be standing right there by the kitchen counter, and... well, I eat so fast. I hardly even taste the food before the next bite is already in my mouth. \n\nIt sounds just terrible, doesn't it? I feel so unladylike and embarrassed even admitting it out loud to you. But I promise you, it's only during those little spells. Any other time, I sit down with a proper plate and napkin. I do hope you don't think I've lost all my dignity... I really am a very proper person normally, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, when these episodes happen, do you tend to eat alone because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no... I wouldn't say I hide away to do it, Doctor. I really wouldn't want you to think I'm being sneaky or... or deceitful in any way. I've always tried to be a very open and honest person. \n\nIt's just that... well, I live by myself now, you see. So I'm almost always alone in the house anyway when it happens. It's not that I'm waiting for people to leave, or locking the doors because I'm ashamed of the amount... it just sort of happens when it's just me and the quiet. If a neighbor were to pop by right then, I would certainly stop and put the kettle on for tea, like a proper hostess. I don't shut the blinds or do anything dreadful like that. \n\nThat makes sense, doesn't it? I do hope you believe me that I'm not trying to be secretive. I'd hate for you to think badly of me, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, aside from feeling embarrassed afterward, do these episodes ever make you feel anxious, guilty, or depressed while they’re happening or afterward?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or anything quite so dramatic as depressed, Doctor. I really try to keep a positive outlook on life! I count my blessings every day, I truly do. \n\nBut... well, if I'm being perfectly honest, which I always try to be with you... I do feel a bit sad afterward. And, well, a little ashamed. You see, cooking and eating... it always used to be about family for me. It was about caring for my late husband and the children, putting a nice meal on the table. And now... well, it feels like I've turned something that was so lovely and full of love into... into this thing I do alone. \n\nIt makes me feel like I have a secret I need to keep from the ladies at church, and I just hate feeling that way. I've always been such an open book. It's just a silly bit of sadness, though. I usually brush myself off and get right back to my day. That's the right way to handle it, isn't it, Doctor? I don't want you to think I'm sitting around feeling sorry for myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, do these episodes of eating more than you intend tend to happen when you're feeling especially sad, lonely, or upset?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... I wouldn't say that at all, Doctor. I really try my best not to let my moods get the better of me. I've always believed that you just have to put on a brave face and keep busy, you know? \n\nI don't... I don't sit down and eat just because I'm feeling a bit lonely or down. manifestation: This started after my close friend died, and it has been going on for about three months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 78 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S050\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, do these episodes of eating more than you intend tend to happen when you're feeling especially sad, lonely, or ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh... well, "complete lack of control" sounds a bit dramatic, doesn't it, Doctor? I wouldn't want you to picture me completely losing my senses or anything like that. I'm usually very put-together. 

But... well, I suppose... when I'm having one of those nice plates of food, like the rice and beans I mentioned... I do sometimes find that... well, I'll tell myself, 'Maria, that is quite enough now.' I really do try to be sensible about it. But then... it's the strangest thing... my hand just sort of goes back to the pot. 

It's almost like... well, like I just can't quite make myself stop in that moment, even though I know I should. It's just because the food is so comforting, you know? It's not a terrible thing to enjoy a good meal, is it? I do hope you don't think I'm completely out of control... it's just a little struggle sometimes, that's all. I'm sure other people have a hard time putting the spoon down when something tastes lovely, don't they?

I hardly even taste the food before the next bite is already in my mouth. \n\nIt sounds just terrible, doesn't it? I feel so unladylike and embarrassed even admitting it out loud to you. I really am a very proper person normally, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, when these episodes happen, do you tend to eat alone because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no... If a neighbor were to pop by right then, I would certainly stop and put the kettle on for tea, like a proper hostess. I don't shut the blinds or do anything dreadful like that. \n\nThat makes sense, doesn't it? I do hope you believe me that I'm not trying to be secretive. That sounds so dreadful. \n\nI just... well, I just sit in my chair until the feeling passes. I might skip my next meal or just have a little cup of tea later on, but that's all. I don't do any of those extreme things, I promise. I know I eat too much sometimes, but I wouldn't do anything unnatural to my body afterward. I hope that's a good thing? I mean, I hope that's the right answer... I'm trying to be very honest with you.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, how often do these episodes of eating more than you intend happen? Would you say they occur several times a week, once a week, or less often?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, I certainly don't want you to think I'm just sitting around the house eating all day long. It's not like that, I promise. But... well, since my dear friend passed away, I suppose it has been happening most weeks. \n\nJust... maybe once or twice a week, really. It always seems to be on those quiet afternoons, you know? Like after I come home from choir, or when I finish up a bit of gardening, and the house is just so still... I don't mean to do it, and it really is just once or twice. Is... is that a very terrible amount, Doctor? I do hope I'm not doing too badly. I really am trying my best to be good about it.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, do these episodes of eating more than you intend cause you a lot of distress, or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I wouldn't say it ruins my life, Doctor. I really wouldn't. I mean, I still keep up with all my responsibilities! I go to Mass every Sunday, I still go to my choir practices, and my house is always neat and tidy, I promise you that. I don't let it stop me from doing what I need to do.\n\nBut... well, I suppose it does bother me a little bit. Sometimes, if I've had one of those... those afternoons where I eat too much, I might just stay inside for the rest of the day. I might not answer the phone if a friend calls, just because... well, I feel so silly and embarrassed about what I've done. I just want to be alone until the feeling passes. But that's not too awful, is it? I mean, everyone needs a quiet evening to themselves sometimes, don't they? I hope you don't think it means I'm falling apart, Doctor. I'm really doing quite well otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes when you eat more than you intend, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... well, I've always prided myself on having good manners, Doctor. I really have. All my life I've been such a slow eater, taking my time like a proper lady should. \n\nBut... oh dear, I suppose I need to be completely honest with you. Lately, when... when this happens, I do find myself rushing. I don't even sit down at the table. I'll just be standing right there by the kitchen counter, and... well, I eat so fast. I hardly even taste the food before the next bite is already in my mouth. \n\nIt sounds just terrible, doesn't it? I feel so unladylike and embarrassed even admitting it out loud to you. But I promise you, it's only during those little spells. Any other time, I sit down with a proper plate and napkin. I do hope you don't think I've lost all my dignity... I really am a very proper person normally, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, when these episodes happen, do you tend to eat alone because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no... I wouldn't say I hide away to do it, Doctor. I really wouldn't want you to think I'm being sneaky or... or deceitful in any way. I've always tried to be a very open and honest person. \n\nIt's just that... well, I live by myself now, you see. So I'm almost always alone in the house anyway when it happens. It's not that I'm waiting for people to leave, or locking the doors because I'm ashamed of the amount... it just sort of happens when it's just me and the quiet. If a neighbor were to pop by right then, I would certainly stop and put the kettle on for tea, like a proper hostess. I don't shut the blinds or do anything dreadful like that. \n\nThat makes sense, doesn't it? I do hope you believe me that I'm not trying to be secretive. I'd hate for you to think badly of me, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, aside from feeling embarrassed afterward, do these episodes ever make you feel anxious, guilty, or depressed while they’re happening or afterward?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or anything quite so dramatic as depressed, Doctor. I really try to keep a positive outlook on life! I count my blessings every day, I truly do. \n\nBut... well, if I'm being perfectly honest, which I always try to be with you... I do feel a bit sad afterward. And, well, a little ashamed. You see, cooking and eating... it always used to be about family for me. It was about caring for my late husband and the children, putting a nice meal on the table. And now... well, it feels like I've turned something that was so lovely and full of love into... into this thing I do alone. \n\nIt makes me feel like I have a secret I need to keep from the ladies at church, and I just hate feeling that way. I've always been such an open book. It's just a silly bit of sadness, though. I usually brush myself off and get right back to my day. That's the right way to handle it, isn't it, Doctor? I don't want you to think I'm sitting around feeling sorry for myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, do these episodes of eating more than you intend tend to happen when you're feeling especially sad, lonely, or upset?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... I wouldn't say that at all, Doctor. I really try my best not to let my moods get the better of me. I've always believed that you just have to put on a brave face and keep busy, you know? \n\nI don't... I don't sit down and eat just because I'm feeling a bit lonely or down. That sounds so... well, so weak, doesn't it? And I really do pride myself on being strong. I keep up with my knitting, I watch my programs... I don't let myself wallow. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say there's any sort of connection like that. It just... happens sometimes, out of the blue. It's not because I'm upset. I do hope you believe me on that, Doctor. I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who can't handle her own feelings... because I handle them quite well, don't I?\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes when you eat more than you intend, do you ever notice that you're eating a very large amount of food in a short time, like within just a couple of hours?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, I suppose... I mean, I wouldn't call it a \"very large amount,\" Doctor. That makes it sound so... gluttonous, doesn't it? I really am quite sensible most of the time. I wouldn't want you to picture me just endlessly stuffing myself. \n\nBut... well, sometimes, say, on a Tuesday afternoon after choir practice, or if I've been out working in the garden and I've worked up a bit of an appetite... I might fix myself a nice plate of rice and beans, or maybe some of my chicken stew. And then... well, I might just go back for a little more. And perhaps a bit more after that. \n\nBefore I realize it, I suppose I might have eaten what you could call... well, maybe enough for two or three meals, just in that afternoon. But it's only because the food is there and it tastes so good, you know? It's not like I'm trying to be greedy. That's just a normal thing to do when you've had a busy day, isn't it, Doctor? I'm sure plenty of people have a hearty appetite after gardening. You don't think too poorly of me for that, do you?\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, when these eating episodes happen, do you ever feel a sense of emotional discomfort or distress that stays with you for a while afterward, even after you've brushed it off?\n\nPatient: Oh, distress? Goodness, no. I wouldn't use a word like that, Doctor. That sounds so... well, so heavy and serious. \n\nLike I mentioned before, I really just try to brush myself off. I don't let things linger or ruin my day. Once the kitchen is cleaned up and the dishes are put away, I just... I put it out of my mind. I might say a little prayer, and then I get right back to my knitting or my chores. It really doesn't stay with me at all. \n\nI've always believed it's best not to dwell on things, you know? If you sit around worrying, you just make yourself miserable, and I've always tried to be a cheerful person for the people around me. It's much better to just keep moving forward with a smile and focus on the good things. That's the healthiest way to be, don't you think? I wouldn't want you to worry that I'm at home agonizing over things.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes, would you say that you feel a complete lack of control over your eating, like you can't stop eating even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, \"complete lack of control\" sounds a bit dramatic, doesn't it, Doctor? I wouldn't want you to picture me completely losing my senses or anything like that. I'm usually very put-together. \n\nBut... well, I suppose... when I'm having one of those nice plates of food, like the rice and beans I mentioned... I do sometimes find that... well, I'll tell myself, 'Maria, that is quite enough now.' I really do try to be sensible about it. But then... it's the strangest thing... my hand just sort of goes back to the pot. \n\nIt's almost like... well, like I just can't quite make myself stop in that moment, even though I know I should. It's just because the food is so comforting, you know? It's not a terrible thing to enjoy a good meal, is it? I do hope you don't think I'm completely out of control... it's just a little struggle sometimes, that's all. I'm sure other people have a hard time putting the spoon down when something tastes lovely, don't they?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Oh, emotionally upset? Goodness, no. I really don't think so, Doctor. 

I mean... I try very hard not to be the sort of person who gets all worked up or unhappy about things. I like to keep a peaceful heart, you know? So I wouldn't say I'm upset when I... well, when I have those extra helpings. Honestly, I haven't noticed any connection like that at all. 

It usually just happens because the food is sitting right there on the stove, and it just tastes so wonderful. A person can just enjoy a nice meal without there being a sad reason behind it, can't they? I just wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting at home crying into my supper or anything dramatic like that. I'm really a very content person, Doctor, I promise you. That's a good way to be, don't you think?

It always seems to be on those quiet afternoons, you know? Like after I come home from choir, or when I finish up a bit of gardening, and the house is just so still... I don't mean to do it, and it really is just once or twice. Is... is that a very terrible amount, Doctor? I do hope I'm not doing too badly. I really am trying my best to be good about it.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, do these episodes of eating more than you intend cause you a lot of distress, or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I wouldn't say it ruins my life, Doctor. I really wouldn't. I mean, I still keep up with all my responsibilities! I go to Mass every Sunday, I still go to my choir practices, and my house is always neat and tidy, I promise you that. I don't let it stop me from doing what I need to do.\n\nBut... well, I suppose it does bother me a little bit. Sometimes, if I've had one of those... those afternoons where I eat too much, I might just stay inside for the rest of the day. I might not answer the phone if a friend calls, just because... well, I feel so silly and embarrassed about what I've done. I just want to be alone until the feeling passes. But that's not too awful, is it? I mean, everyone needs a quiet evening to themselves sometimes, don't they? I hope you don't think it means I'm falling apart, Doctor. I'm really doing quite well otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes when you eat more than you intend, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... well, I've always prided myself on having good manners, Doctor. I really have. All my life I've been such a slow eater, taking my time like a proper lady should. \n\nBut... oh dear, I suppose I need to be completely honest with you. Lately, when... when this happens, I do find myself rushing. I don't even sit down at the table. I'll just be standing right there by the kitchen counter, and... well, I eat so fast. I hardly even taste the food before the next bite is already in my mouth. \n\nIt sounds just terrible, doesn't it? I feel so unladylike and embarrassed even admitting it out loud to you. But I promise you, it's only during those little spells. Any other time, I sit down with a proper plate and napkin. I do hope you don't think I've lost all my dignity... I really am a very proper person normally, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, when these episodes happen, do you tend to eat alone because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no... I wouldn't say I hide away to do it, Doctor. I really wouldn't want you to think I'm being sneaky or... or deceitful in any way. I've always tried to be a very open and honest person. \n\nIt's just that... well, I live by myself now, you see. So I'm almost always alone in the house anyway when it happens. It's not that I'm waiting for people to leave, or locking the doors because I'm ashamed of the amount... it just sort of happens when it's just me and the quiet. If a neighbor were to pop by right then, I would certainly stop and put the kettle on for tea, like a proper hostess. I don't shut the blinds or do anything dreadful like that. \n\nThat makes sense, doesn't it? I do hope you believe me that I'm not trying to be secretive. I'd hate for you to think badly of me, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, aside from feeling embarrassed afterward, do these episodes ever make you feel anxious, guilty, or depressed while they’re happening or afterward?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or anything quite so dramatic as depressed, Doctor. I really try to keep a positive outlook on life! I count my blessings every day, I truly do. \n\nBut... well, if I'm being perfectly honest, which I always try to be with you... I do feel a bit sad afterward. And, well, a little ashamed. You see, cooking and eating... it always used to be about family for me. It was about caring for my late husband and the children, putting a nice meal on the table. And now... well, it feels like I've turned something that was so lovely and full of love into... into this thing I do alone. \n\nIt makes me feel like I have a secret I need to keep from the ladies at church, and I just hate feeling that way. I've always been such an open book. It's just a silly bit of sadness, though. I usually brush myself off and get right back to my day. That's the right way to handle it, isn't it, Doctor? I don't want you to think I'm sitting around feeling sorry for myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, do these episodes of eating more than you intend tend to happen when you're feeling especially sad, lonely, or upset?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... I wouldn't say that at all, Doctor. I really try my best not to let my moods get the better of me. I've always believed that you just have to put on a brave face and keep busy, you know? \n\nI don't... I don't sit down and eat just because I'm feeling a bit lonely or down. That sounds so... well, so weak, doesn't it? And I really do pride myself on being strong. I keep up with my knitting, I watch my programs... I don't let myself wallow. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say there's any sort of connection like that. It just... happens sometimes, out of the blue. It's not because I'm upset. I do hope you believe me on that, Doctor. I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who can't handle her own feelings... because I handle them quite well, don't I?\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes when you eat more than you intend, do you ever notice that you're eating a very large amount of food in a short time, like within just a couple of hours?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, I suppose... I mean, I wouldn't call it a \"very large amount,\" Doctor. That makes it sound so... gluttonous, doesn't it? I really am quite sensible most of the time. I wouldn't want you to picture me just endlessly stuffing myself. \n\nBut... well, sometimes, say, on a Tuesday afternoon after choir practice, or if I've been out working in the garden and I've worked up a bit of an appetite... I might fix myself a nice plate of rice and beans, or maybe some of my chicken stew. And then... well, I might just go back for a little more. And perhaps a bit more after that. \n\nBefore I realize it, I suppose I might have eaten what you could call... well, maybe enough for two or three meals, just in that afternoon. But it's only because the food is there and it tastes so good, you know? It's not like I'm trying to be greedy. That's just a normal thing to do when you've had a busy day, isn't it, Doctor? I'm sure plenty of people have a hearty appetite after gardening. You don't think too poorly of me for that, do you?\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, when these eating episodes happen, do you ever feel a sense of emotional discomfort or distress that stays with you for a while afterward, even after you've brushed it off?\n\nPatient: Oh, distress? Goodness, no. I wouldn't use a word like that, Doctor. That sounds so... well, so heavy and serious. \n\nLike I mentioned before, I really just try to brush myself off. I don't let things linger or ruin my day. Once the kitchen is cleaned up and the dishes are put away, I just... I put it out of my mind. I might say a little prayer, and then I get right back to my knitting or my chores. It really doesn't stay with me at all. \n\nI've always believed it's best not to dwell on things, you know? If you sit around worrying, you just make yourself miserable, and I've always tried to be a cheerful person for the people around me. It's much better to just keep moving forward with a smile and focus on the good things. That's the healthiest way to be, don't you think? I wouldn't want you to worry that I'm at home agonizing over things.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes, would you say that you feel a complete lack of control over your eating, like you can't stop eating even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, \"complete lack of control\" sounds a bit dramatic, doesn't it, Doctor? I wouldn't want you to picture me completely losing my senses or anything like that. I'm usually very put-together. \n\nBut... well, I suppose... when I'm having one of those nice plates of food, like the rice and beans I mentioned... I do sometimes find that... well, I'll tell myself, 'Maria, that is quite enough now.' I really do try to be sensible about it. But then... it's the strangest thing... my hand just sort of goes back to the pot. \n\nIt's almost like... well, like I just can't quite make myself stop in that moment, even though I know I should. It's just because the food is so comforting, you know? It's not a terrible thing to enjoy a good meal, is it? I do hope you don't think I'm completely out of control... it's just a little struggle sometimes, that's all. I'm sure other people have a hard time putting the spoon down when something tastes lovely, don't they?\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, would you say that these episodes happen more often when you're feeling emotionally upset, even if you don't dwell on it afterward?\n\nPatient: Oh, emotionally upset? Goodness, no. I really don't think so, Doctor. \n\nI mean... I try very hard not to be the sort of person who gets all worked up or unhappy about things. I like to keep a peaceful heart, you know? So I wouldn't say I'm upset when I... well, when I have those extra helpings. Honestly, I haven't noticed any connection like that at all. \n\nIt usually just happens because the food is sitting right there on the stove, and it just tastes so wonderful. A person can just enjoy a nice meal without there being a sad reason behind it, can't they? I just wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting at home crying into my supper or anything dramatic like that. I'm really a very content person, Doctor, I promise you. That's a good way to be, don't you think?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, my... well, I don't know if I'd compare myself to *most* people, Doctor. I wouldn't want you to think I'm being greedy or anything unseemly like that. We all enjoy a good, hearty meal sometimes, don't we? 

But... I suppose if I'm being perfectly honest... some afternoons, especially after I've been at choir practice or working out in the garden, I'll fix myself a nice plate of rice and beans, or maybe some of my chicken stew. And... well, I just keep going back to the stove for a little bit more, and then a little bit more. If I really stop and think about it, by the time I'm finished, I suppose I might have eaten enough for... well, maybe enough for two or three meals. 

It just tastes so comforting, and I was raised never to let good food go to waste. That's a sensible way to look at it, isn't it? I do hope you don't judge me too harshly for having a big appetite on those days.

I don't do any of those extreme things, I promise. I know I eat too much sometimes, but I wouldn't do anything unnatural to my body afterward. I hope that's a good thing? I mean, I hope that's the right answer... I'm trying to be very honest with you.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, how often do these episodes of eating more than you intend happen? Would you say they occur several times a week, once a week, or less often?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, I certainly don't want you to think I'm just sitting around the house eating all day long. It's not like that, I promise. But... well, since my dear friend passed away, I suppose it has been happening most weeks. \n\nJust... maybe once or twice a week, really. It always seems to be on those quiet afternoons, you know? Like after I come home from choir, or when I finish up a bit of gardening, and the house is just so still... I don't mean to do it, and it really is just once or twice. Is... is that a very terrible amount, Doctor? I do hope I'm not doing too badly. I really am trying my best to be good about it.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, do these episodes of eating more than you intend cause you a lot of distress, or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I wouldn't say it ruins my life, Doctor. I really wouldn't. I mean, I still keep up with all my responsibilities! I go to Mass every Sunday, I still go to my choir practices, and my house is always neat and tidy, I promise you that. I don't let it stop me from doing what I need to do.\n\nBut... well, I suppose it does bother me a little bit. Sometimes, if I've had one of those... those afternoons where I eat too much, I might just stay inside for the rest of the day. I might not answer the phone if a friend calls, just because... well, I feel so silly and embarrassed about what I've done. I just want to be alone until the feeling passes. But that's not too awful, is it? I mean, everyone needs a quiet evening to themselves sometimes, don't they? I hope you don't think it means I'm falling apart, Doctor. I'm really doing quite well otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes when you eat more than you intend, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... well, I've always prided myself on having good manners, Doctor. I really have. All my life I've been such a slow eater, taking my time like a proper lady should. \n\nBut... oh dear, I suppose I need to be completely honest with you. Lately, when... when this happens, I do find myself rushing. I don't even sit down at the table. I'll just be standing right there by the kitchen counter, and... well, I eat so fast. I hardly even taste the food before the next bite is already in my mouth. \n\nIt sounds just terrible, doesn't it? I feel so unladylike and embarrassed even admitting it out loud to you. But I promise you, it's only during those little spells. Any other time, I sit down with a proper plate and napkin. I do hope you don't think I've lost all my dignity... I really am a very proper person normally, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, when these episodes happen, do you tend to eat alone because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no... I wouldn't say I hide away to do it, Doctor. I really wouldn't want you to think I'm being sneaky or... or deceitful in any way. I've always tried to be a very open and honest person. \n\nIt's just that... well, I live by myself now, you see. So I'm almost always alone in the house anyway when it happens. It's not that I'm waiting for people to leave, or locking the doors because I'm ashamed of the amount... it just sort of happens when it's just me and the quiet. If a neighbor were to pop by right then, I would certainly stop and put the kettle on for tea, like a proper hostess. I don't shut the blinds or do anything dreadful like that. \n\nThat makes sense, doesn't it? I do hope you believe me that I'm not trying to be secretive. I'd hate for you to think badly of me, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, aside from feeling embarrassed afterward, do these episodes ever make you feel anxious, guilty, or depressed while they’re happening or afterward?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or anything quite so dramatic as depressed, Doctor. I really try to keep a positive outlook on life! I count my blessings every day, I truly do. \n\nBut... well, if I'm being perfectly honest, which I always try to be with you... I do feel a bit sad afterward. And, well, a little ashamed. You see, cooking and eating... it always used to be about family for me. It was about caring for my late husband and the children, putting a nice meal on the table. And now... well, it feels like I've turned something that was so lovely and full of love into... into this thing I do alone. \n\nIt makes me feel like I have a secret I need to keep from the ladies at church, and I just hate feeling that way. I've always been such an open book. It's just a silly bit of sadness, though. I usually brush myself off and get right back to my day. That's the right way to handle it, isn't it, Doctor? I don't want you to think I'm sitting around feeling sorry for myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, do these episodes of eating more than you intend tend to happen when you're feeling especially sad, lonely, or upset?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... I wouldn't say that at all, Doctor. I really try my best not to let my moods get the better of me. I've always believed that you just have to put on a brave face and keep busy, you know? \n\nI don't... I don't sit down and eat just because I'm feeling a bit lonely or down. That sounds so... well, so weak, doesn't it? And I really do pride myself on being strong. I keep up with my knitting, I watch my programs... I don't let myself wallow. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say there's any sort of connection like that. It just... happens sometimes, out of the blue. It's not because I'm upset. I do hope you believe me on that, Doctor. I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who can't handle her own feelings... because I handle them quite well, don't I?\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes when you eat more than you intend, do you ever notice that you're eating a very large amount of food in a short time, like within just a couple of hours?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, I suppose... I mean, I wouldn't call it a \"very large amount,\" Doctor. That makes it sound so... gluttonous, doesn't it? I really am quite sensible most of the time. I wouldn't want you to picture me just endlessly stuffing myself. \n\nBut... well, sometimes, say, on a Tuesday afternoon after choir practice, or if I've been out working in the garden and I've worked up a bit of an appetite... I might fix myself a nice plate of rice and beans, or maybe some of my chicken stew. And then... well, I might just go back for a little more. And perhaps a bit more after that. \n\nBefore I realize it, I suppose I might have eaten what you could call... well, maybe enough for two or three meals, just in that afternoon. But it's only because the food is there and it tastes so good, you know? It's not like I'm trying to be greedy. That's just a normal thing to do when you've had a busy day, isn't it, Doctor? I'm sure plenty of people have a hearty appetite after gardening. You don't think too poorly of me for that, do you?\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, when these eating episodes happen, do you ever feel a sense of emotional discomfort or distress that stays with you for a while afterward, even after you've brushed it off?\n\nPatient: Oh, distress? Goodness, no. I wouldn't use a word like that, Doctor. That sounds so... well, so heavy and serious. \n\nLike I mentioned before, I really just try to brush myself off. I don't let things linger or ruin my day. Once the kitchen is cleaned up and the dishes are put away, I just... I put it out of my mind. I might say a little prayer, and then I get right back to my knitting or my chores. It really doesn't stay with me at all. \n\nI've always believed it's best not to dwell on things, you know? If you sit around worrying, you just make yourself miserable, and I've always tried to be a cheerful person for the people around me. It's much better to just keep moving forward with a smile and focus on the good things. That's the healthiest way to be, don't you think? I wouldn't want you to worry that I'm at home agonizing over things.\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes, would you say that you feel a complete lack of control over your eating, like you can't stop eating even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, \"complete lack of control\" sounds a bit dramatic, doesn't it, Doctor? I wouldn't want you to picture me completely losing my senses or anything like that. I'm usually very put-together. \n\nBut... well, I suppose... when I'm having one of those nice plates of food, like the rice and beans I mentioned... I do sometimes find that... well, I'll tell myself, 'Maria, that is quite enough now.' I really do try to be sensible about it. But then... it's the strangest thing... my hand just sort of goes back to the pot. \n\nIt's almost like... well, like I just can't quite make myself stop in that moment, even though I know I should. It's just because the food is so comforting, you know? It's not a terrible thing to enjoy a good meal, is it? I do hope you don't think I'm completely out of control... it's just a little struggle sometimes, that's all. I'm sure other people have a hard time putting the spoon down when something tastes lovely, don't they?\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, would you say that these episodes happen more often when you're feeling emotionally upset, even if you don't dwell on it afterward?\n\nPatient: Oh, emotionally upset? Goodness, no. I really don't think so, Doctor. \n\nI mean... I try very hard not to be the sort of person who gets all worked up or unhappy about things. I like to keep a peaceful heart, you know? So I wouldn't say I'm upset when I... well, when I have those extra helpings. Honestly, I haven't noticed any connection like that at all. \n\nIt usually just happens because the food is sitting right there on the stove, and it just tastes so wonderful. A person can just enjoy a nice meal without there being a sad reason behind it, can't they? I just wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting at home crying into my supper or anything dramatic like that. I'm really a very content person, Doctor, I promise you. That's a good way to be, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Mrs. Maria, during these episodes, would you say that you are eating an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most people would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... well, I don't know if I'd compare myself to *most* people, Doctor. I wouldn't want you to think I'm being greedy or anything unseemly like that. We all enjoy a good, hearty meal sometimes, don't we? \n\nBut... I suppose if I'm being perfectly honest... some afternoons, especially after I've been at choir practice or working out in the garden, I'll fix myself a nice plate of rice and beans, or maybe some of my chicken stew. And... well, I just keep going back to the stove for a little bit more, and then a little bit more. If I really stop and think about it, by the time I'm finished, I suppose I might have eaten enough for... well, maybe enough for two or three meals. \n\nIt just tastes so comforting, and I was raised never to let good food go to waste. That's a sensible way to look at it, isn't it? I do hope you don't judge me too harshly for having a big appetite on those days."
